[low] Indexing-speed extrapolation for the 8640HS (6 cores, 28 W) from the i7-13700K figures: MiniLM fp32 roughly 400-600 chunks/s, bge-small 200-300 chunks/s; int8 ONNX typically adds 1.5-3x on CPU. For 100k emails at ~3 chunks each (300k chunks) that is roughly 10-15 min (MiniLM int8) to 20-30 min (bge-small), and seconds for potion/static models. Not measured on the target hardware (this session's machine is a 24-core desktop and no packages were installed to benchmark).
     -> Embedding is not the ingest bottleneck; OCR (easyocr) and especially the PRD's per-email LLM triple extraction will be. Run embedding in the background sync with a thread pool of ~4 and batch size 32-64.
  22. [medium] Brute-force query cost: 300k x 384 fp32 = 461 MB (fp16 230 MB, int8 115 MB); a single matrix-vector pass is memory-bandwidth-bound, so ~10-30 ms per query at laptop DRAM speeds, ~8-20 ms for 200k vectors; sqlite-vec's measured 33 ms for 1M x 128-d (same byte volume as ~330k x 384) corroborates. HNSW only becomes necessary past roughly 500k chunks.
     -> Plain numpy (np.memmap of fp16, cosine via normalized dot) or sqlite-vec is sufficient for the PRD's 10k-100k email range and keeps RAM well under the 16 GB shared budget alongside a ~6 GB LLM; keep an HNSW tier (usearch/voyager) behind an extra.
  23. [high] Metadata-filter capability matrix for the PRD's 'range + equality + AND/OR': Chroma $and/$or/$gt/$gte/$lte/$in — yes; LanceDB SQL WHERE with AND/OR/NOT/LIKE/IN and prefilter — yes; sqlite-vec vec0 KNN WHERE — equality, ranges, IN, AND yes, OR/LIKE no (workaround: SQL -> rowid IN (...)); faiss — any predicate via precomputed IDSelector; usearch Python — none (post-filter); numpy — any boolean mask.
     -> Keep all email metadata in SQLite regardless of vector engine; derive the candidate id set with ordinary SQL (dates, sender, project, folder, OR/LIKE) and intersect with the vector scan — this makes the vector engine swappable.
  24. [medium] Zero-network hygiene for the embedding stack: huggingface-hub (pulled by fastembed, model2vec, tokenizers) will attempt Hub calls unless models are loaded from explicit local paths or HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1 is set; huggingface_hub also documents HF_HUB_DISABLE_TELEMETRY. Chroma's only residual artifact is the local telemetry_user_id file.
     -> Set HF_HUB_OFFLINE=1 and HF_HUB_DISABLE_TELEMETRY=1 at process start, load every model by absolute path from the installed wheel, and add an automated test that fails if any socket other than 127.0.0.1 is opened during indexing/search.
  25. [low] Chroma 1.x Windows import failures are rare; the one forum report of 'DLL load failed while importing chromadb_rust_bindings' was resolved by installing the VC++ redistributable, which python.org CPython already requires/bundles (vcruntime140).
     -> Add an install-time self-check (`import chromadb_rust_bindings` / `import onnxruntime`) with a clear message rather than relying on users to diagnose DLL errors.


### Verifier
recommendation_holds=True

**Revised recommendation:** The recommendation holds and is slightly strengthened. Core: stdlib SQLite (python.org Windows CPython ships SQLite 3.50.4 with FTS5 enabled and enable_load_extension available - verified locally on 3.14.0) for metadata + FTS5, vectors in sqlite-vec 0.1.9 (py3-none-win_amd64, 0.3 MB, zero deps, pre-v1 brute force) or an fp16 numpy memmap, embeddings from a bundled int8 all-MiniLM-L6-v2 ONNX (23.0 MB, Apache-2.0) via onnxruntime 1.31.0 + tokenizers 0.23.3, shipped as a separate PyPI wheel (well under the 100 MB per-file limit) so install is pip-only and offline. Because vec0 KNN WHERE supports only =, !=, <, <=, >, >=, IN and AND (no OR/LIKE/NULL/scalar functions), do the PRD's OR/LIKE/date filters in plain SQL first and pass the result as `rowid IN (...)`, which sqlite-vec handles natively. Keep chromadb out of the core: independently re-resolved at 85 packages / 82 MB, its default EF needs an 83 MB S3 download, and the only public Windows import failure was fixed by installing the VC++ Redistributable, which needs admin rights - a direct conflict with the no-admin constraint; offer it only as an optional [chroma] backend fed by your own embedder. Two corrections to the researcher's facts: chroma-hnswlib 0.7.6 has Windows wheels only for cp37-cp311 (not cp312), reinforcing that hnswlib-based paths are dead on modern Python; and the indexing-time and query-latency figures are unmeasured extrapolations that must be benchmarked on the 8640HS. Tiers: [lite] model2vec potion-base-8M (24 pkgs / 22 MB) or the sbert static-retrieval model; [bigindex] usearch 2.26.5 / voyager 2.2.2 (post-filter only) or vectorlite-py if staying inside SQLite is preferred; [lancedb] 18 pkgs / 129 MB with real SQL WHERE (OR/LIKE/IN, prefilter default) and native FTS as the heavier alternative; [gpu] onnxruntime-directml only as an opt-in experiment; sentence-transformers (42 pkgs / 217 MB) developer-only.

  - UNCERTAIN: 25. Chroma 1.x Windows import failures are rare; the one forum report of 'DLL load failed while importing chromadb_rust_bindings' was resolved by installing the
      evidence: The inflearn thread (poster on 2026-08-18, fresh Windows 11 Pro 25H2 + python.org Python 3.12.10, chromadb version not stated) does report 'ImportError: DLL load failed while importing chromadb_rust_bindings' and the poster's own fix was installing the Microsoft Visual C++ Redistributable. Two web searches found no other report and no chroma GitHub issue, so 'rare' is unfalsified but based on one data point. The inference that the redistributable is 'already' present is contradicted by this very case: a clean Windows 11 + python.org Python install did NOT have what the Rust extension needed. python.org installers ship vcruntime140.dll privately for the interpreter but not the full VC++ redistributable (e.g. msvcp140.dll), and installing the redistributable system-wide requires administrator rights. Chroma's troubleshooting page has no DLL entry (only an hnswlib build-error link and a SQLite DLL replacement tip).
      corrected: One public report (fresh Windows 11 25H2, python.org Python 3.12.10) shows chromadb's Rust extension failing to import until the Microsoft VC++ Redistributable was installed; python.org Python does not guarantee that redistributable is present, and installing it needs admin rights. For a no-admin corporate laptop this is a real (if apparently uncommon) deployment risk for chromadb, whereas sqlite-vec (pure C, py3-none wheel) and numpy carry no such dependency.

### Missed Options

  - vectorlite-py 0.2.0 (sqlite loadable extension, py3-none-win_amd64 wheel 0.8 MB, zero Python deps): HNSW (hnswlib) inside SQLite with `rowid IN (...)` metadata filter pushed down into graph traversal, so a >500k tier could stay in the same SQLite file as FTS5/metadata instead of adding usearch/voyager index-file lifecycle. Caveat: PyPI wheel dated 2024-08-19 while the GitHub README describes a Rust rewrite, so release cadence must be checked. https://pypi.org/pypi/vectorlite-py/json, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/1yefuwang1/vectorlite/main/README.md
  - qdrant-client 1.20.0 local mode (py3-none-any 0.5 MB; deps grpcio, httpx[http2], numpy, portalocker, protobuf, pydantic, urllib3): `QdrantClient(path=...)` runs the full Qdrant filter DSL (must/should/range/match, i.e. AND/OR/ranges) fully offline with no server, as a drop-in `[qdrant]` backend behind the swappable interface. Docs scope it to 'testing things out and debugging or if you plan to store just a small amount of vectors', so a prototyping/compat tier, not core. https://pypi.org/pypi/qdrant-client/json, https://raw.githubusercontent.com/qdrant/qdrant-client/master/README.md
  - tantivy 0.27.0 (cp311-abi3 win_amd64 4.3 MB, zero deps) or bm25s 0.3.15 (pure Python + numpy) as keyword-search alternatives if FTS5 proves limiting (fuzzy/phrase/BM25 tuning). Verified locally that the python.org Windows CPython 3.14 build has SQLite 3.50.4 with ENABLE_FTS5=1, so stdlib FTS5 needs no extra wheel and should remain the default. https://pypi.org/pypi/tantivy/json, https://pypi.org/pypi/bm25s/json
  - sentence-transformers/static-retrieval-mrl-en-v1 as the `[lite]` static embedder instead of potion-base-8M: NanoBEIR NDCG@10 0.5032 vs MiniLM 0.5623 (potion-base-8M MTEB retrieval 31.1 vs MiniLM 42.9 is a larger gap), 107k sent/s on CPU, Apache-2.0, and runnable with tokenizers + numpy (embedding-bag mean) without torch; weight-file size and licence should be verified via the HF API before adopting. https://huggingface.co/blog/static-embeddings
  - onnxruntime-directml 1.24.4 as an optional `[gpu]` extra for the embedder on the Radeon 760M: confirmed cp311-cp314 win_amd64 wheels, DX12-only requirement; but DirectML is now in 'sustained engineering' (new work moved to WinML), the package lags onnxruntime by 7 minor versions, and it does not support multi-threaded Run on one session, so it should never be the default path. https://pypi.org/pypi/onnxruntime-directml/json, https://onnxruntime.ai/docs/execution-providers/DirectML-ExecutionProvider.html
  - Rejected after checking (so the researcher was right to skip them): duckdb 2.0.0 (15.4 MB abi3 wheel, but VSS/FTS are runtime-downloaded extensions, not offline-friendly); annoy 1.17.3 (sdist only); pynndescent (numba/llvmlite); txtai 9.14 (requires torch + transformers).



---

## Area: knowledge-graph


### Summary

Per-email LLM triple extraction with Llama 3 8B Q4_0 is not viable as the default ingestion path on this laptop: measured Vulkan numbers on the same iGPU class (760M: 8B Q8_0 at pp512 236 t/s, tg128 9.8 t/s; 780M-class: 7B Q4_0 at pp ~270-310 t/s, tg ~20 t/s) and CPU-only numbers on the sibling 7840U (7B Q8_0 pp ~45-50 t/s, tg ~7.7 t/s; Ollama llama3.1:8b ~10.8 t/s) put one 400-in/100-out extraction at roughly 9 s on the iGPU and 20-25 s on CPU, i.e. ~400 emails/h (GPU) or ~160 emails/h (CPU); 20k emails is ~2 days of continuous iGPU time or ~5 days of CPU time, and GPT4All's Kompute path is unbenchmarked relative to llama.cpp Vulkan. A deterministic graph from .msg headers (sender -> to/cc, thread via PidTagConversationIndex 0x0071 / inReplyTo / messageId, attachments, org domains) costs zero inference and extract-msg 0.56.1 exposes every field needed; spaCy en_core_web_sm (12 MB, NER F 84.3, ~10k words/s CPU) adds entity nodes for the whole 20k corpus in minutes, and GLiNER small-v2.1 ONNX int8 (183 MB, Apache-2.0) is the step-up option. networkx 3.7 handles 100k nodes / 1M edges in ~383 MB with sub-millisecond 2-hop BFS (local measurement) and pickle round-trips in seconds, but GraphML is 131 MB and ~60 s to write. pyvis is abandoned (last commit Feb 2023, 0.3.2) and its default cdn_resources='local' still loads vis-network from cdnjs and bootstrap from jsdelivr, so it breaks air-gapped; the correct move is to bundle vis-network (435 KB) or sigma.js+graphology (~470 KB) inside the library's own web UI and emit JSON. For GraphRAG, use NER/fuzzy entity linking from the query -> bounded 2-hop neighborhood (cap nodes) -> optional PPR on the subgraph, with token budgets copied from Microsoft GraphRAG local search defaults (top_k_entities 10, top_k_relationships 10, max_context_tokens 12000).


### Recommended

Build the knowledge graph deterministically from .msg metadata (sender -> recipients/CC, thread edges from PidTagConversationIndex/inReplyTo/messageId, attachments, org domains) plus spaCy en_core_web_sm entity nodes and entity co-mention edges, stored in networkx in memory and persisted as pickle or SQLite; this runs on all 20k emails in minutes, needs ~45-65 MB of pip packages, and gives useful GraphRAG with zero LLM cost. Offer GLiNER small-v2.1 ONNX (Apache-2.0) as an optional '[ner-zeroshot]' extra for schema-free types, and demote LLM triple extraction to an opt-in, resumable, per-project or on-demand enrichment since the 760M sustains only ~400 extractions/hour. Replace pyvis with vis-network (<=2k nodes, physics) or sigma.js (larger) bundled in the library's own web UI, fed by JSON for a bounded 2-hop neighbourhood of the query entities.


### Alternatives

  - PRD as written (GPT4All Llama 3 8B triple extraction per email + pyvis) — richest relation types but ~2 days of iGPU time (or ~5 days CPU) per 20k emails, dormant engine limited to Q4_0/Q4_1 on Vulkan, and pyvis output needs a CDN unless in_line; only sane as an opt-in subset job.
  - Llama 3.2 3B Q4_0 for extraction instead of 8B — ~2.4x faster (~20 h per 20k emails on the 760M), 1.9 GB weights, but noisier triples and still a multi-hour background job.
  - GLiNER2.5-small (74M) via gliner2[local] — smallest zero-shot NER+relation model for CPU, Apache-2.0 code, but base install is a cloud-API client and weight licensing is unstated.
  - gliner-relex-large-v1.0 (Apache-2.0, ~0.5B) for typed relations without an LLM — pip-installable but ~2 GB fp32 and slow on CPU; REBEL/GLiREL/ReLiK are faster to set up but CC-BY-NC-SA (unusable in a corporate product).
  - rustworkx or igraph instead of networkx — 2-3 MB abi3 Windows wheels, far lower memory per edge, but less ergonomic attribute handling and fewer GraphRAG recipes; only needed beyond ~1M edges.
  - Cytoscape.js (WebGL preview since 3.31) instead of sigma/vis-network — best algorithm toolkit in-browser, WebGL still marked experimental.


### Install Footprint

Recommended (graph core): networkx 2.1 MB + spaCy 15.2 MB + thinc/blis/srsly/cymem/preshed/murmurhash ~8.5 MB + pydantic/typer/etc ~5-10 MB + en_core_web_sm 12 MB (or md 31 MB) + bundled vis-network 0.44 MB or sigma+graphology 0.47 MB = roughly 45-65 MB download; add scipy (~40 MB) only if nx.pagerank is used. Optional zero-shot NER extra: gliner 0.26 MB + torch 124 MB (win_amd64) + transformers (~10 MB) + onnxruntime 14.9 MB + gliner_small-v2.1 int8 183 MB (fp16 306 MB) = ~330-460 MB. PRD option: gpt4all 119.6 MB win wheel + Meta-Llama-3-8B-Instruct.Q4_0.gguf 4.66 GB + pyvis 0.76 MB (+ IPython/jedi ~5 MB) + networkx 2.1 MB = ~4.8 GB; swapping to Llama 3.2 3B Q4_0 (1.92 GB) makes it ~2.05 GB.


### Open Questions For Owner

  - Is an LLM-extracted semantic graph a must-have for v1, or is a header/thread/NER graph acceptable with LLM enrichment as an opt-in per-project job that may take hours to days?
  - Does the corporate proxy allow downloads from github.com (needed for spaCy model wheels) in addition to pypi.org, or must language models be vendored into your own PyPI packages?
  - Are the target laptops soldered LPDDR5X-7500 or SODIMM DDR5-5600? (~25% difference in LLM decode speed; also determines whether the 16 GB can be shared with OCR and the LLM concurrently.)
  - What is the acceptable license set for bundled models? Apache-2.0 only (spaCy models are MIT, GLiNER v2.1 Apache-2.0) excludes REBEL/GLiREL/ReLiK (CC-BY-NC-SA) and requires checking GLiNER2 weight terms.
  - Which graph views does the UI need: bounded neighbourhoods of a query/entity (<=2k nodes, vis-network is enough) or whole-project overviews of 5k-20k nodes (needs sigma.js/WebGL and precomputed layouts)?
  - Should the graph store live in SQLite (incremental upsert aligned with the 1:1 mirror sync and pruning) or as a pickled networkx object rewritten on each sync?
  - If LLM extraction stays, do you accept Llama 3.2 3B (1.9 GB, ~2.4x faster) as the default enrichment model instead of Llama 3 8B?


### Claims

  1. [high] llama.cpp Vulkan on a Radeon 760M (Ryzen 5 7640HS, same 8-CU RDNA3 iGPU as the 8640HS) measured llama 8B Q8_0 at pp512 235.96 t/s and tg128 9.80 t/s; a 26B-A4B MoE Q4_K_M decoded at 20.9 t/s on the same box, showing decode is bandwidth-bound.
     -> Expect Llama 3 8B Q4_0 (4.66 GB, ~59% of Q8_0 bytes) to decode at roughly 12-16 t/s and prefill at ~200-250 t/s on the 760M under llama.cpp Vulkan; plan ingestion budgets on those numbers, not on desktop GPU folklore.
  2. [medium] The llama.cpp Vulkan scoreboard (standard test: llama 7B Q4_0) lists 'AMD Ryzen 8000 Series' iGPU at pp512 267-311 t/s and tg128 19.8-21.3 t/s, 'Ryzen 7000 Series' at 282/19.9, and warns that for integrated graphics memory speed and channel count dominate.
     -> The 780M-class iGPU ceiling for an 8B Q4 model is ~17-19 t/s decode; the 760M has 8 CU vs 12 CU so prefill will be ~1/3 slower while decode stays close (bandwidth-bound). Soldered LPDDR5x-7500 vs DDR5-5600 SODIMM changes decode by ~25%.
  3. [medium] CPU-only llama-bench on a Ryzen 7 7840U (8 threads) gives llama-2 7B Q8_0 pp512 44.7-49.8 t/s and tg128 7.6-7.7 t/s; Ollama CPU-only on the same chip reported llama3.1:8b at 10.81 t/s (Jan 2025) and llama3:8b at 5.64 t/s (Jun 2024).
     -> On the 6-core 8640HS expect ~35-45 t/s prefill and ~8-10 t/s decode for 8B Q4_0 CPU-only. Any CPU-only fallback for the LLM extraction path is 2-3x slower than the iGPU path and will contend with the user's foreground work.
  4. [medium] Derived throughput: with ~400 input + 100 output tokens per email, one extraction costs ~9 s on the 760M (1.8 s prefill + ~7 s decode) and ~22 s CPU-only (10 s + 12.5 s). That is ~400 emails/h (iGPU) or ~160 emails/h (CPU); 20k emails = ~50 h continuous iGPU (~2.1 days, ~6 working days at 8 h/day) or ~125 h CPU (~5.2 days, ~16 working days). Adding a 400-token instruction/few-shot prefix without KV prefix caching adds 1.8 s (GPU) or 10 s (CPU) per email. Llama 3.2 3B Q4_0 (1.92 GB) would be ~2.4x faster (~20 h for 20k on iGPU) at lower extraction quality.
     -> Per-email LLM triple extraction cannot be the default ingestion step. Make it an opt-in, resumable, low-priority background enrichment over a subset (e.g. emails matching a project filter, or on-demand per thread), and keep the instruction prefix byte-identical so any prefix KV cache is hit.
  5. [high] GPT4All Python bindings are at 2.8.2 (uploaded 2024-08-14; win_amd64 wheel 119.6 MB); the desktop app's last release is v3.10.0 (Feb 2025); the last non-CI commit on main is Feb 25 2025 and the only later commit is a CI bump on May 27 2025. The README states Vulkan GPU support covers 'Q4_0 and Q4_1 quantizations in GGUF'; device defaults to 'cpu' on Windows, 'amd' selects 'the best GPU provided by the Kompute backend from this vendor', and an insufficient-VRAM device throws and invalidates the instance. No benchmark comparing GPT4All's Kompute backend to llama.cpp's Vulkan backend on an iGPU was found.
     -> The PRD's LLM engine is effectively dormant (20+ months without a release) and restricts GPU use to Q4_0/Q4_1. Treat the throughput figures above as an upper bound for GPT4All, and keep the LLM engine behind an optional extra so the graph/search product does not depend on it. (llama-cpp-python 0.3.36 ships only an sdist on PyPI, so it is not a drop-in pip-only replacement on a no-compiler laptop.)
  6. [high] extract-msg 0.56.1 (2026-08-14) exposes sender, to, cc, bcc, recipients (List[Recipient]), date, receivedTime, messageId (from the transport header, else stream 1035), inReplyTo (stream 1042), header/headerDict (an email.message.Message, so 'References' is available when transport headers exist), attachments, and a generic getPropertyVal(tag) for any MAPI property; it has no named ConversationIndex property.
     -> A deterministic graph (person -> SENT/RECEIVED/CC -> email, email -> REPLIES_TO -> email, email -> HAS_ATTACHMENT -> file, person -> MEMBER_OF -> domain) is fully buildable with zero inference. Read PidTagConversationIndex via msg.getPropertyVal('00710102') as the primary thread key, since internal Exchange mail saved as .msg often lacks Message-ID/In-Reply-To transport headers.
  7. [high] PidTagConversationIndex (0x0071, PtypBinary) is a 22-byte header (reserved byte, 5-byte FILETIME, 16-byte GUID generated per new conversation thread) followed by one 5-byte response-level block per reply, so the first 22 bytes identify the thread and the block count gives reply depth.
     -> Thread edges can be derived deterministically from the GUID prefix plus prefix-matching of the index bytes (parent = longest proper prefix present in the corpus), with no dependency on RFC headers.
  8. [high] spaCy 3.8.16 (2026-08-24) ships cp310-cp314 win_amd64 wheels (15.2 MB; thinc 1.3 MB, blis 6.2 MB, srsly 0.7 MB). en_core_web_sm 3.8.0 is 12 MB with NER F 84.33 and en_core_web_md 3.8.0 is 31 MB with NER F 84.73; both are pure-Python wheels hosted on GitHub Releases (not PyPI). spaCy's own benchmark lists en_core_web_lg at 10,014 words/s on CPU.
     -> spaCy NER over 20k emails (~6M words) is roughly 10 minutes of CPU, fits the no-admin/pip-only constraint, and produces PERSON/ORG/GPE/DATE/MONEY nodes. Because the model is not on PyPI, either vendor it inside your own optional wheel (e.g. emailsupersearch-models-en) or run 'spacy download' at install time; a corporate proxy that allows pypi.org but blocks github.com is a real risk.
  9. [high] GLiNER 0.2.29 (2026-09-08) hard-depends on torch>=2.0.0 and transformers even for ONNX use (onnxruntime is only the [onnx] extra); from_pretrained accepts load_onnx_model=True / onnx_model_file / runtime='onnxruntime'|'openvino'. torch 2.14.1 win_amd64 is 124 MB, onnxruntime 1.31.0 win_amd64 is 14.9 MB (cp311-cp314). urchade/gliner_small-v2.1 is 166M params, Apache-2.0; onnx-community/gliner_small-v2.1 offers model.onnx 611 MB, fp16 306 MB, int8/uint8/quantized 183 MB, q4f16 245 MB.
     -> GLiNER gives zero-shot, schema-free entity types (project, vendor, invoice number, system) that spaCy cannot, but the pip footprint is ~330 MB (torch pulled in regardless) plus a 183-306 MB model. Package it as a separate optional extra ('[ner-zeroshot]') and default to spaCy.
  10. [low] No verified CPU latency for GLiNER small ONNX int8 was found; the GLiNER2 paper reports CPU classification latency of 130-208 ms per text (5-50 labels), and a community INT8 build warns that full INT8 activation quantization of the DeBERTa-v3-small backbone hurts recall (weight-only INT8 avoids it).
     -> Budget ~0.2-0.5 s per email for GLiNER-small on CPU (20k emails = ~1-3 h, still 20-50x cheaper than the LLM path) and benchmark fp16/weight-only-int8 before shipping int8; measure on the target laptop before committing.
  11. [high] Relation-extraction model licenses: REBEL (Babelscape/rebel-large, ~0.4B params F32, BART seq2seq, 200+ relation types) is CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0; GLiREL (jackboyla/glirel-large-v0; PyPI glirel 1.2.1, 2025-04-11, depends on torch+transformers) is CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0; ReLiK (PyPI 1.0.7, 2024-09-12) requires python<3.12 and pins torch==2.3.1, and its data/software are CC-BY-NC-SA-4.0. Apache-2.0 options are knowledgator/gliner-relex-large-v1.0 (~0.5B params, joint NER+RE in one pass via the gliner library) and knowledgator/gliner-multitask-large-v0.5 (relations prompted as 'entity <> relation').
     -> REBEL, GLiREL and ReLiK are non-commercial licensed and ReLiK's pins are incompatible with a modern Python; exclude them from a product. If typed semantic relations beyond headers are wanted, gliner-relex-large (Apache-2.0, ~2 GB fp32) is the only pip-installable candidate, and it is a large CPU model; most GraphRAG value comes from header edges + co-mention edges anyway.
  12. [medium] GLiNER2 (PyPI gliner2 2.1.0, 2026-10-09, Apache-2.0 code) has a base install with no torch that is a cloud API client requiring FASTINO_API_KEY; local inference needs 'gliner2[local]' (torch+transformers). Models: gliner2.5-small-v1 74M params ('fast CPU/edge'), gliner2.5-base-v1 194M, gliner2-base-v1 205M; it supports NER, classification, structured extraction and relation extraction; weight licenses are not stated on the README.
     -> gliner2.5-small (74M) is the smallest credible zero-shot NER+relation extractor for CPU, but the default pip install would silently route to an external API, which violates the zero-telemetry rule; if adopted, depend on gliner2[local] explicitly and verify weight licensing.
  13. [high] Local measurement (networkx 3.7, Python 3.14.0, Windows 11, this session): a DiGraph with 100,000 attributed nodes and 1,000,000 attributed edges built in 4.8 s using 383 MB (tracemalloc); 2-hop BFS over both directions from 3 seeds reached 1,572 nodes in 0.8 ms and the induced subgraph (1,786 edges) in 18 ms; nx.pagerank with personalization over the full graph took 9.9 s and requires scipy; pickle protocol 5 wrote 38 MB in 1.0 s and loaded in 5.6 s; GraphML wrote 131 MB in 60.7 s and read in 42.9 s; node_link_data JSON was 69 MB in 6.5 s. write_gpickle no longer exists (removed in networkx 3.0, PR #5773). The 2016 SNAP paper measured NetworkX at ~426 bytes/edge and ~1158 bytes/node.
     -> networkx is adequate in-memory for the stated scale (~0.4-0.5 GB for 100k/1M) and k-hop expansion is instant; persist with pickle (or SQLite tables for nodes/edges with incremental upsert for the 1:1 mirror sync), never GraphML at this size. Run PPR only on the bounded k-hop subgraph, not the full graph. If memory must drop, rustworkx 0.18.1 (2.3 MB abi3 win wheel) or igraph 1.0.0 (3.2 MB) are pip-only alternatives.
  14. [high] networkx release 3.7 (2026-09-21) requires Python >=3.12 (and !=3.14.1); 3.5/3.6 require >=3.11; 3.4.2 requires >=3.10. All are pure-Python wheels (~2 MB). pagerank and most matrix algorithms import scipy at call time.
     -> Pin 'networkx>=3.3' rather than '==3.7' so the library installs on a corporate Python 3.10/3.11; declare scipy only in the graph extra if you use nx.pagerank, or implement a 20-line power iteration on the subgraph to avoid the ~40 MB scipy wheel.
  15. [high] pyvis is unmaintained: latest PyPI release 0.3.2 (2023-02-24), last commit on master 2023-02-10, top-level 'from IPython.display import IFrame' makes IPython a hard dependency. Its constructor defaults to cdn_resources='local', but the shipped template.html's 'local' branch still loads vis-network 9.1.2 CSS/JS from cdnjs.cloudflare.com (only utils.js and tom-select are served from ./lib), 'remote' loads from cdnjs, and only 'in_line' embeds lib/vis-9.1.2/vis-network.min.js (468,813 bytes) and vis-network.css (220,163 bytes). Bootstrap 5.0.0-beta3 CSS/JS is loaded from cdn.jsdelivr.net unconditionally in every mode.
     -> As specified in the PRD, pyvis output will fail to render on an air-gapped laptop (blank canvas) unless cdn_resources='in_line' is used, and even then it makes blocked requests for bootstrap and emits ~0.7 MB of inlined libs per file plus data. Drop pyvis; the library already has a web UI, so serve a bundled renderer and feed it JSON.
  16. [high] vis-network's README states it 'works smooth on any modern browser for up to a few thousand nodes and edges' and offers clustering for larger graphs; current npm release is 10.1.2 (Apache-2.0 OR MIT), vis-network.min.js is 435 KB (peer build) or 652 KB (standalone UMD).
     -> The PRD's top-500 node cap is conservative and correct for vis-network with physics enabled; raise to ~2,000 nodes only with physics disabled after stabilization. Bundle the 435-652 KB file in the wheel under the package's static folder; both licenses permit redistribution.
  17. [high] sigma.js describes itself as 'aimed at visualizing graphs of thousands of nodes and edges using WebGL', is MIT, built on graphology (MIT); sigma@4 min.js is 392 KB and graphology UMD min is 74 KB. Cytoscape.js 3.31.0 (Jan 2025) added a preview WebGL renderer; the team's benchmark on an M1 MacBook showed ~1,200 nodes/16,000 edges going from ~20 FPS (canvas) to 100+ FPS (WebGL); cytoscape.min.js 3.34.4 is 436 KB, MIT.
     -> If the UI should show whole-project graphs of 5k-20k nodes, bundle sigma.js + graphology (+ ForceAtlas2 worker, layout computed in-browser or precomputed in Python) instead of vis-network; for <=2k-node neighbourhood views vis-network's built-in physics and simpler API suffice. All candidates are <1 MB and MIT/Apache, so the choice does not affect install footprint.
  18. [high] Microsoft GraphRAG local search identifies entities semantically related to the query via entity-description embeddings, then pulls text units, relationships, community reports and covariates, ranks/filters them 'to fit within a single context window of pre-defined size'. Code defaults: text_unit_prop 0.5, community_prop 0.15, conversation_history_max_turns 5, top_k_entities 10, top_k_relationships 10, max_context_tokens 12,000.
     -> Copy the budgeting scheme, not the pipeline: embed entity labels into a small Chroma collection, map the query to top-10 entities, take top-10 relationships per entity, and fill a fixed token budget (e.g. 50% email snippets, 35% graph triples, 15% thread summary) sized to the local model's n_ctx (GPT4All default 2048; set 4096-8192 and keep total context <= ~3k tokens to protect prefill latency).
  19. [high] HippoRAG retrieves by extracting named entities from the query, linking each to its most similar KG node with a retrieval encoder, running Personalized PageRank with equal personalization mass on the query nodes (weighted by node specificity = 1/number of passages the node appears in), then ranking passages by the summed PPR score of their nodes; indexing uses LLM OpenIE plus synonymy edges at cosine >= 0.8. LightRAG uses dual-level (entity / relation) retrieval with explicit token budgets MAX_ENTITY_TOKENS, MAX_RELATION_TOKENS, MAX_TOTAL_TOKENS and requires an LLM 'KEYWORD' call per query.
     -> The robust zero-LLM pattern is: spaCy/GLiNER NER + rapidfuzz match on the query -> seed nodes -> 2-hop BFS capped at N nodes (measured <1 ms) -> PPR on that subgraph (node specificity = 1/email-count) -> top-k emails by summed score, merged with Chroma hits via reciprocal rank fusion. Avoid LightRAG-style LLM keyword extraction at query time: on this hardware it adds 5-10 s per question.
  20. [medium] Ryzen 5 PRO 8640HS: 6 cores/12 threads, 3.5/4.9 GHz, 28 W, Hawk Point (Zen 4), Radeon 760M = 8 CUs / 512 shaders at up to 2.6 GHz (vs 780M 12 CU, 740M 4 CU), 128-bit DDR5/LPDDR5X bus; the APU family supports up to LPDDR5X-7500 (~120 GB/s) or DDR5-5600 (~90 GB/s).
     -> Decode speed for any model is capped by ~90-120 GB/s shared memory: 4.66 GB of Q4_0 weights implies a hard ceiling of ~19-25 t/s and a practical 12-16 t/s on the 760M. Whether the laptop has soldered LPDDR5X or SODIMM DDR5 is a 25% swing worth detecting at runtime.
  21. [high] Llama 3 8B Instruct Q4_0 as shipped in GPT4All's catalog is 4.66 GB on disk with 'ramrequired' 8 GB; Llama 3.2 3B Q4_0 is 1.92 GB (4 GB RAM) and 1B is 0.77 GB.
     -> With 16 GB shared UMA, the 8B model plus KV cache plus Chroma/ONNX/OCR processes leaves little headroom for the background sync to also run OCR concurrently; serialize LLM extraction behind OCR/embedding, or offer the 3B model as the default 'graph enrichment' model.


### Verifier
recommendation_holds=True

**Revised recommendation:** The recommendation holds. Build the graph deterministically from .msg metadata (sender/recipients/CC via the MAPI recipient table, thread edges from PidTagConversationIndex read through extract-msg getPropertyVal('00710102') with References/In-Reply-To/ConversationTopic fallbacks, attachments, org domains) plus spaCy en_core_web_sm entities (spaCy 3.8.16 + thinc 8.3.x + blis + srsly ~25 MB of wheels, model 12.8 MB) and co-mention edges. Keep networkx (pin <3.7 if Python 3.11 must be supported) for in-memory algorithms, but persist the authoritative graph in SQLite tables so the background 1:1 mirror can insert/delete per message; a pickle cache is fine (load measured 0.5 s for 1M edges). For zero-shot typing, prefer GLiNER2.5-small-v1 (74M, Apache-2.0 weights confirmed on HF, NER + relations) as the optional extra if a torch dependency is acceptable, otherwise gliner_small-v2.1 ONNX fp16/weight-only-INT8 (avoid dynamic INT8: recall halves in the one available test; expect roughly 250-500 ms per email on a laptop CPU). Keep LLM triple extraction opt-in and resumable: realistic 760M throughput is 300-480 emails/h (42-67 h per 20k) using llama.cpp Vulkan numbers as a proxy, GPT4All's Kompute backend is unbenchmarked on this iGPU, and a single-channel DDR5 corporate laptop could halve decode speed. Drop pyvis (CDN loads in every mode except in_line, Bootstrap always from CDN, IPython dependency, unmaintained since Feb 2023) in favour of vis-network 10.1.2 (412-435 KB, Apache-2.0/MIT) bundled in the wheel, serving a JSON 2-hop neighbourhood capped at ~2k nodes, with sigma.js/graphology as the WebGL path if larger views are ever needed.

      evidence: The arithmetic does not follow directly from the cited Q8_0 numbers: 400 tokens / 235.96 t/s = 1.7 s prefill, but 100 tokens / 9.80 t/s = 10.2 s decode, i.e. ~12 s/email (~300/h), not 9 s. The ~7 s decode is only reachable by assuming Q4_0 decodes ~1.5-1.7x faster than Q8_0 (weights 4.66 GB vs 7.95 GB; the scoreboard's 7B Q4_0 ~20 t/s on Ryzen 8000 iGPU supports ~17-18 t/s for 8B Q4_0 -> ~6 s). So the defensible iGPU range is ~7.5-12 s/email = 300-480 emails/h = 42-67 h for 20k. CPU: pp 45-50 t/s -> 8-9 s prefill; tg 7.7 t/s Q8_0 (Q4_0 plausibly 10-12) -> 8-13 s decode -> 17-22 s/email = 160-210/h = 95-125 h. Unverified assumptions: 100 output tokens is low for JSON triple lists; GPT4All's Kompute backend has no published iGPU benchmark; whether GPT4All reuses the KV prefix across calls is undocumented. The 2.4x for 3B matches the weight ratio 4.66/1.92 = 2.43 for decode only; prefill does not scale that way. Order of magnitude (tens of hours iGPU, days CPU) stands.

CPU-only is ~17-22 s/email (160-210/h, ~95-125 h). These are llama.cpp Vulkan numbers on Linux; GPT4All's Kompute backend is unbenchmarked on this iGPU, and 100 output tokens per email is optimistic for triple JSON.

### Missed Options

  - Persist the graph in SQLite (stdlib, zero deps) as node/edge tables with recursive CTEs for k-hop neighbourhoods instead of a pickled networkx object: a 1:1 mirror of a .msg folder needs incremental inserts/deletes per file, which a whole-graph pickle rewrite handles poorly; load into networkx only for algorithms (pickle load of the 1M-edge graph measured 0.5 s, so a cache is cheap either way).
  - Ladybug (formerly Kuzu; kuzudb/kuzu archived 2025-10-10 at v0.11.3, MIT; successor PyPI 'real-ladybug' 0.15.3, 2026-04-01, MIT, win_amd64 wheels cp310-cp313): embedded Cypher graph DB with built-in vector + full-text indexes, viable as the single store for graph + embeddings, at the cost of a native dependency and a young fork (https://github.com/kuzudb/kuzu, https://pypi.org/pypi/real-ladybug/json).
  - HippoRAG-style synonymy/alias edges using the embedding model already shipped for chromadb (cosine >= 0.8 between entity-name embeddings) to merge 'J. Smith' / 'John Smith' / 'jsmith@corp' without any LLM; plus deterministic alias resolution from the MAPI recipient table (display name <-> SMTP address pairs are in every .msg).
  - GLiNER2.5-small-v1 (74M, DeBERTa-v3-xsmall, Apache-2.0 weights on HF, joint NER + relation extraction, CPU-oriented) via gliner2[local] as the optional zero-shot extra instead of gliner_small-v2.1 ONNX: it covers relations too and the licence concern is resolved; trade-off is a hard torch dependency (~124 MB wheel) and no ONNX export in gliner2.
  - spaCy EntityRuler / regex patterns for office-domain identifiers (ticket IDs, project codes, PO numbers, document names) as deterministic entity nodes - these are typically the most useful 'entities' in corporate email and need no model.
  - Thread fallbacks beyond ConversationIndex: PidTagConversationTopic (0x0070) + normalised subject, and the RFC 'References' header chain, since messages from non-Exchange senders may lack PidTagConversationIndex.
  - Community detection for GraphRAG-style summaries without external deps: networkx.community.louvain_communities (pure Python, no scipy) or leidenalg 0.12.0 (abi3 win_amd64 wheel, but GPL-3 - a licensing issue for a corporate product); graspologic 3.4.4 only supports Python <3.13.
  - Pin strategy: networkx 3.7 requires Python >=3.12, so a library targeting Python 3.11 corporate installs must pin networkx>=3.4.2,<3.7; onnxruntime 1.31.0 requires >=3.11.
  - Lighter visualisation for the bounded 2-hop view: d3-force (ISC, ~90 KB) or vis-network's peer build (412 KB) bundled as a static file in the library's wheel; Cytoscape.js WebGL is still a 'preview' with provisional API.



---

## Area: ocr-attachments


### Summary

The PRD's easyocr + PyMuPDF choice is the heaviest and legally riskiest option available: easyocr 1.7.2 (last release Sep 2024) pulls torch (124 MB CPU wheel) plus ~100 MB of other wheels and then downloads ~91 MB of weights from GitHub on first Reader(), runs ~2 s/page on a desktop i7 CPU, and has no AMD-GPU path on Windows; PyMuPDF 2.0 is AGPL-3/commercial, now 63 MB, and hard-depends on its own onnxruntime. Windows' inbox OCR (Windows.Media.Ocr via the winrt-Windows.Media.Ocr wheels, ~1 MB total, MIT) needs zero model downloads, ran a 1654x2339 page in 0.13 s with 0.17% CER in a local test, and Windows 11 lets non-admin users add OCR language features unless IT sets RestrictLanguagePacksAndFeaturesInstall. rapidocr 3.10 ships PP-OCRv6 multilingual ONNX models inside its 27 MB wheel (verified by unzip), needs only onnxruntime (Microsoft-signed DLLs), is Apache-2, and gave 0.03% CER at ~2.6 s/page locally, making it the right fully-offline, multi-language fallback. tesserocr now has Windows wheels bundling Tesseract, but its unsigned DLLs were blocked by Smart App Control on the test machine, a realistic corporate WDAC/AppLocker hazard. For PDFs, pypdfium2 (BSD/Apache, 4 MB) + pdfplumber/pdfminer.six (MIT) cover text and page rendering without AGPL; note that extract-msg itself is GPL-3, which constrains the library's own license regardless of the OCR choice.


### Recommended

Make Windows.Media.Ocr (via the winrt-Windows.Media.Ocr family, ~1 MB, MIT, zero downloads, ~0.1 s/page, 0.17% CER locally) the default OCR backend on Windows, selected automatically when OcrEngine.AvailableRecognizerLanguages contains the project's language, and ship rapidocr + onnxruntime as the `[ocr]` extra (≈90 MB of wheels, Apache-2, PP-OCRv6 multilingual models bundled inside the wheel, Microsoft-signed runtime, fully offline, ~3-6 s/page on the target CPU) as the fallback when the language pack is missing, blocked by RestrictLanguagePacksAndFeaturesInstall, or when the user needs CJK/other scripts. Drop easyocr (torch + ~91 MB GitHub downloads, 2-year-old release, slowest CPU path, no AMD GPU) and PaddleOCR. For attachments use pypdfium2 for PDF text and page rasterisation, pdfplumber/pdfminer.six optionally for layout, python-docx/openpyxl/python-pptx/xlrd for Office, stdlib email for .eml, Pillow for images, and extract-msg recursion for nested .msg; remove PyMuPDF (AGPL, 63 MB, its own onnxruntime) so the only copyleft dependency left is extract-msg (GPL-3), which the product owner must consciously accept in the library's own license.


### Alternatives

  - easyocr (PRD choice) — Apache-2 and multilingual with solid scene-text robustness, but ~190-225 MB of wheels (torch 124 MB) plus 91 MB of weights downloaded from GitHub on first use, ~2 s/page on a desktop i7 CPU and no AMD-GPU path on Windows; last release Sep 2024.
  - tesserocr wheels (Tesseract 5 bundled) — 23 MB wheel + 4 MB tessdata_fast per language, MIT, fast and accurate on clean print, but unsigned fresh DLLs were blocked by Smart App Control on the test machine (likely under corporate WDAC too) and traineddata must be bundled or pre-seeded.
  - PaddleOCR 3.x (PP-OCRv5/v6 server models) — highest accuracy ceiling, but ~105 MB paddlepaddle (no cp314) + paddlex, models pulled from HuggingFace/BOS after a connectivity check at init, and strictly heavier than running the same models through rapidocr/onnxruntime.
  - OnnxTR (docTR on onnxruntime) — Apache-2, ~0.57 s/page CPU and good on Latin-script documents, but models are fetched from the network at first use and must be pre-seeded; smaller language coverage than PP-OCRv6.
  - PyMuPDF with an Artifex commercial licence — fastest single-dependency PDF/text/render engine (and optional Tesseract hookup), at the cost of per-deployment licensing, a 63 MB wheel and a bundled second onnxruntime; AGPL route would force the whole library to AGPL-3.


### Install Footprint

Recommended core (no OCR extra): pypdfium2 3.95 MB + pdfminer.six 6.6 MB + pdfplumber 0.06 MB + pypdf 0.41 MB + python-docx 0.25 MB + openpyxl 0.25 MB + python-pptx 0.47 MB + xlrd 0.1 MB + extract-msg 0.34 MB + Pillow 7.3 MB + lxml (~4 MB) ≈ 24 MB of wheels, zero runtime downloads. `[ocr-win]` (default on Windows): winrt-runtime 0.24 MB + six winrt-Windows.* wheels ≈ 1 MB, no models. `[ocr]` fallback: rapidocr 27.3 MB (31.75 MB of ONNX models inside) + onnxruntime 14.9 MB + opencv-python-headless 43.8 MB + Shapely/pyclipper/omegaconf/colorlog ≈ 90 MB download, ~130 MB on disk, zero runtime downloads; optional onnxruntime-directml 25 MB instead of onnxruntime for the Radeon 760M. PRD option: easyocr 2.9 MB + torch 124.1 MB + torchvision 1.4 MB + opencv-python-headless 43.8 MB + scikit-image 11.9 MB + scipy/numpy/Shapely/pyclipper/python-bidi (~50 MB) ≈ 230 MB of wheels, plus 91.3 MB of model zips (craft_mlt_25k 77.25 MB + english_g2 14.04 MB; latin_g2 another 14.28 MB) downloaded from GitHub into ~/.EasyOCR at first Reader(); PyMuPDF 2.0 adds a 63 MB wheel + onnxruntime 14.9 MB + numpy/networkx/psutil/tabulate/pymupdf4llm ≈ 85 MB versus ≈ 11 MB for pypdfium2 + pdfplumber + pdfminer.six. PaddleOCR for comparison: paddlepaddle 105 MB + paddlex + 21 MB (mobile) or 165 MB (server) of models fetched at first run.


### Open Questions For Owner

  - Which OCR languages must be supported (English only, English + European Latin-script languages, or CJK/Cyrillic/Arabic)? This decides whether Windows OCR alone suffices and which rapidocr models, if any, must be pre-bundled.
  - What license will eMailSuperSearch itself carry? extract-msg is GPL-3 (and PyMuPDF would be AGPL-3), so a permissive MIT/Apache release requires replacing extract-msg; keeping it means publishing under GPL-3/AGPL-3.
  - Are target laptops under WDAC/AppLocker/Smart App Control policies that block unsigned native DLLs? Can someone run a 5-line import test (onnxruntime, cv2, winrt, pypdfium2) on a real corporate device before the stack is frozen?
  - Does the corporate Windows image ship the OCR language feature for the users' languages, and is RestrictLanguagePacksAndFeaturesInstall set? This determines whether the rapidocr fallback is optional or mandatory.
  - Should OCR of scanned pages run inline during ingestion or as a deferred low-priority pass with a per-page time budget and a 'pending OCR' state visible in the UI?
  - Should inline images in email bodies (logos, signatures, screenshots) be OCRed, or only true attachments? Inline OCR adds noise and CPU time but may capture screenshots of text.
  - How should legacy/unsupported formats (.doc, .ppt, .xls beyond xlrd, .zip, .odt) be handled: skip, index filename/metadata only, or recursively unpack archives?


### Claims

  1. [high] easyocr latest is 1.7.2 (uploaded 2024-09-24, Apache-2.0, 2.87 MB pure-python wheel) and requires torch, torchvision>=0.5, opencv-python-headless, scipy, numpy, Pillow, scikit-image, python-bidi, PyYAML, Shapely, pyclipper and ninja; there has been no release in over two years.
     -> Treat easyocr as a maintenance risk and a large transitive dependency tree; if kept at all, isolate it behind an optional extra.
  2. [medium] On Windows the easyocr stack costs roughly 190-225 MB of wheels before any model: torch 2.14.1 win_amd64 wheels (cp310-cp314) are 124.1 MB each, torchvision 0.29.1 is 1.4 MB, opencv-python-headless 5.0.0.93 is 43.8 MB, scikit-image 0.26.0 is 11.9 MB (plus scipy). The PyPI torch Windows wheel is the CPU build: its nvidia-*/cuda-* requirements are marker-gated to platform_system == "Linux", and CUDA/ROCm builds come from download.pytorch.org index URLs.
     -> A 'pip install emailsupersearch[ocr]' that drags torch will take minutes on corporate networks and bloat the user profile; the Radeon 760M cannot be used by this torch build anyway.
  3. [high] easyocr downloads models at first Reader() via urllib.request.urlretrieve from GitHub releases: craft_mlt_25k.zip (detector) is 77.25 MB, english_g2.zip 14.04 MB, latin_g2.zip 14.28 MB. Cache dir is ~/.EasyOCR/model, overridable by EASYOCR_MODULE_PATH (or MODULE_PATH) or the model_storage_directory argument; download_enabled=False makes it raise instead of downloading, so weights can be pre-seeded offline.
     -> If easyocr is retained, the library must ship or pre-seed ~91 MB of .pth files into %USERPROFILE% and always construct Reader(download_enabled=False) to honour the zero-external-calls rule.
  4. [medium] Published CPU timings (OnnxTR benchmark, i7-14700K): EasyOCR 1.7.1 ~1.96 s/page on FUNSD and ~1.75 s/page on CORD, versus PyTesseract ~0.50/0.52 s, PaddleOCR 2.7.3 ~1.27/0.38 s, docTR ~1.29/0.60 s and OnnxTR ~0.57/0.25 s. A 6-core Ryzen 5 8640HS will be slower than that desktop CPU, so expect several seconds per scanned page with easyocr.
     -> Scanned-PDF OCR must run as a deferred, throttled background pass regardless of engine; easyocr makes the per-page budget worst.
  5. [high] There is no supported path to accelerate easyocr on the AMD Radeon 760M under Windows: torch-directml's latest release is 0.2.5.dev240914 (Sep 2024), pinned to torch==2.4.1/torchvision==0.19.1 and cp38-cp312 only.
     -> Do not plan on GPU OCR with easyocr; if iGPU acceleration matters, onnxruntime-directml with rapidocr is the viable route.
  6. [high] rapidocr 3.10.0 (uploaded 2026-10-08) is a 27.29 MB universal wheel, requires_python >=3.8,<4; its dependencies are pyclipper, opencv-python-headless, numpy, six, Shapely, PyYAML, Pillow, tqdm, omegaconf, requests, colorlog (python-bidi via the 'rtl' extra). onnxruntime is no longer a declared dependency since 2.0.6 and must be installed alongside (`pip install rapidocr onnxruntime`). Code and upstream PaddleOCR-derived models are Apache-2.0.
     -> Declare an extra like `ocr-rapid = ["rapidocr>=3.10,<4", "onnxruntime>=1.31"]`; permissive licensing is compatible with any library license.
  7. [high] The rapidocr 3.10.0 wheel bundles its default models (verified by listing the wheel): rapidocr/models/PP-OCRv6_det_small.onnx 9.93 MB, PP-OCRv6_rec_small.onnx 21.23 MB, ch_ppocr_mobile_v2.0_cls_mobile.onnx 0.59 MB (31.75 MB uncompressed). Default inference needs no network. Non-default models are hosted on ModelScope (www.modelscope.cn RapidAI/RapidOCR) and can be pre-fetched with `rapidocr download_models` for offline deployment; config exposes model_path/model_dir/model_root_dir.
     -> Default rapidocr satisfies 'zero external calls after pip'; pin to the bundled models and never call the ModelScope download path in production code.
  8. [high] The bundled PP-OCRv6 small recognizer is multilingual: default_models.yaml in the wheel routes ch, chinese_cht, en, japan plus ~50 Latin-script languages (de, fr, es, it, nl, pl, pt, sv, da, fi, cs, hu, ro, tr, vi, ...) to the same model; RTL languages need the 'rtl' extra (rapidocr>=3.10). Chinese and English are the documented defaults.
     -> One bundled model covers English plus most European languages without extra downloads; Cyrillic/Arabic/Devanagari would require pre-seeded additional models.
  9. [high] The legacy package rapidocr-onnxruntime (1.4.4, 2025-01-17) declares requires_python <3.13 and is being phased out in favour of rapidocr.
     -> Use `rapidocr`, not `rapidocr-onnxruntime`, or the OCR extra will be uninstallable on Python 3.13+.
  10. [medium] Local measurement (Windows 11 Home 26200, Intel Core Ultra 9 285K, Python 3.14, onnxruntime 1.31 CPU): rapidocr 3.10 default models on a synthetic 1654x2339 (A4 @ 200 dpi) page of 2,944 characters of 30 px Arial business text: init 0.01 s, 3.34 s first page, 2.64 s second page, CER 0.03%.
     -> Budget roughly 3-6 s per scanned page on the Ryzen 5 8640HS for rapidocr; accuracy on clean print is excellent, so it is a sound fallback engine.
  11. [high] onnxruntime 1.31.0 (2026-10-09, MIT) has win_amd64 wheels for cp311-cp314 at 14.9 MB and its DLLs are Authenticode-signed by Microsoft; onnxruntime-directml 1.24.4 (2026-03-17) provides a DirectML execution provider (25 MB, cp311-cp314) and rapidocr's config has a `use_dml` switch for it.
     -> onnxruntime is the safest native runtime under code-integrity policies; offer onnxruntime-directml as an opt-in extra to use the Radeon 760M, but keep CPU as the default.
  12. [low] No rigorous independent three-way accuracy benchmark of RapidOCR vs EasyOCR vs Tesseract on business documents was found. PaddleOCR's own tables put PP-OCRv5 mobile recognition at 81.29% vs server 86.38% (det Hmean 79.0 vs 83.8). One single-invoice test (Mar 2026) scored Tesseract 87.5%, RapidOCR 75%, EasyOCR 62.5%; academic comparisons describe Tesseract as best on clean print and EasyOCR as stronger on scene text.
     -> Decide the engine on footprint/offline/licensing grounds and validate accuracy on a sample of the user's own scanned attachments; keep the OCR backend pluggable.
  13. [high] winrt-Windows.Media.Ocr 3.2.1 (2025-09-20) ships win_amd64 wheels for cp39-cp314 (~40 KB) and needs winrt-runtime 3.2.1 (0.24 MB, MIT) plus winrt-Windows.Foundation, .Foundation.Collections, .Globalization, .Graphics.Imaging and .Storage.Streams; the whole set is well under 2 MB. winsdk 1.0.0b10 (Aug 2023, cp38-cp312) is the superseded line; winocr 0.0.15 (Oct 2024, 10 KB, MIT) is a thin wrapper that depends on exactly these winrt-* packages.
     -> Depend on the winrt-* packages directly (marker `sys_platform == 'win32'`) and vendor ~40 lines of winocr-style glue rather than depending on winocr or winsdk.
  14. [high] Windows.Media.Ocr.OcrEngine is an inbox Windows 10+ API (windows.dll, UniversalApiContract v1): it recognises only languages whose OCR language feature is installed, exposes AvailableRecognizerLanguages / IsLanguageSupported / TryCreateFromLanguage / TryCreateFromUserProfileLanguages, takes an uncompressed SoftwareBitmap and returns lines of words with bounding rects; there are no model files to download.
     -> The library can probe AvailableRecognizerLanguages at startup and enable the Windows backend automatically when the needed language is present.
  15. [medium] Local measurement (Windows 11 Home 26200, Python 3.14, standard non-elevated process): OcrEngine.max_image_dimension = 10000 pixels (the 2600 px figure often quoted is outdated), available_recognizer_languages = [en-GB] only (the system display language), and recognising the same 1654x2339 synthetic page took 0.13 s cold / 0.12 s warm with CER 0.17%.
     -> Windows OCR is ~20x faster than rapidocr/easyocr on CPU with comparable accuracy on clean print; make it the default engine and still downscale pages so max(w,h) <= OcrEngine.max_image_dimension read at runtime.
  16. [high] Microsoft documents that 'In Windows 11, non-administrator user accounts can add both display language and its corresponding language features' (including OCR) from Settings > Time & Language, but IT admins can block this with the RestrictLanguagePacksAndFeaturesInstall Group Policy/MDM policy; Add-WindowsCapability/DISM require elevation, not all languages have an OCR FoD, and Get-WindowsCapability itself failed locally with 'requires elevation'.
     -> Document the Settings path for users, detect language availability via OcrEngine.AvailableRecognizerLanguages (no DISM), and fall back to rapidocr when the pack is absent or locked down by policy.
  17. [high] Windows OCR practical constraints: the engine needs raw pixel buffers (winocr converts PIL images to RGBA8 and builds a SoftwareBitmap via DataWriter), the API is async (asyncio.run per call works; winocr reports 1000 images in 48 s single-core and 16 s with 4 processes), it returns no per-word confidence, and multi-page TIFF/PDF rasterisation is the caller's job.
     -> Wrap the engine in a worker that rasterises PDF pages with pypdfium2 and iterates TIFF frames with Pillow; parallelise with a small process pool.
  18. [high] pytesseract 0.3.13 (Aug 2024, Apache-2.0) is only a CLI wrapper: 'You must be able to invoke the tesseract command as tesseract', i.e. a separately installed Tesseract binary, which a standard user cannot install via pip.
     -> Exclude pytesseract; it violates the pip-only, no-installer constraint.
  19. [high] tesserocr 2.12.0 (uploaded 2026-10-22, MIT) publishes win_amd64 wheels for cp39-cp315 (~23 MB) that 'bundle Tesseract, Leptonica, and their runtime dependencies' so 'No separate Tesseract installation is required', but 'Language data must still be installed separately' (eng.traineddata: tessdata_fast 4.11 MB, tessdata_best 15.40 MB, tessdata 23.47 MB) via TESSDATA_PREFIX or PyTessBaseAPI(path=...).
     -> tesserocr is pip-only viable (23 MB + a traineddata file you would have to bundle in your own wheel or a companion data package), making it a possible third backend.
  20. [medium] On the test machine (Windows 11 Home with Smart App Control in enforcement mode, registry VerifiedAndReputablePolicyState=1) `import tesserocr` failed with 'DLL load failed while importing tesserocr: An Application Control policy has blocked this file'. Its DLLs/pyd are unsigned and one day old; onnxruntime's DLLs are Microsoft-signed and loaded, and older unsigned but widely used binaries (cv2.pyd, pdfium.dll, Pillow, winrt pyds) also loaded. Microsoft states Smart App Control blocks 'unknown, unsigned code' unless app-intelligence deems it safe.
     -> Corporate WDAC/AppLocker/Smart App Control can silently break any wheel with fresh unsigned native code; prefer inbox (Windows OCR) and Microsoft-signed (onnxruntime) engines, import every native backend lazily inside try/except, and surface a clear diagnostic in the UI.
  21. [high] PaddleOCR 3.7.0 (Jun 2026) depends on paddlex[ocr-core] and PaddlePaddle; paddlepaddle 3.3.1 win_amd64 wheels (cp39-cp313, no cp314) are ~105 MB, require AVX+MKL and the official CPU install uses PaddlePaddle's own index. PaddleX downloads models on first use into ~/.paddlex/official_models (PADDLE_PDX_CACHE_HOME) from HuggingFace (default) or BOS and performs a connectivity check to the model hosters unless PADDLE_PDX_DISABLE_MODEL_SOURCE_CHECK is set. PP-OCRv5 mobile det/rec are 4.7 + 16 MB (CPU ~1.75 s/image), server 84.3 + 81 MB (~4.34 s/image on a Xeon).
     -> PaddleOCR is strictly heavier than rapidocr (which runs the same PP-OCR models on onnxruntime) and phones home at init by default; exclude it.
  22. [high] PyMuPDF 2.0 (uploaded 2026-10-19) is 'Dual Licensed - GNU AFFERO GPL 3.0 or Artifex Commercial License' ('Free for open-source projects', 'separate commercial licences ... for proprietary applications'); the win_amd64 wheel grew from 19.8 MB (1.28.2, Aug 2026) to 63.0 MB (2.0, cp311-abi3), requires Python >=3.11 and now hard-depends on onnxruntime, numpy, networkx, psutil, tabulate, pymupdf4llm==2.0 and an empty pymupdf-layout==2.0 shim because the former pymupdf-layout code (1.26.6: 'Commercial license. See artifex.com'; 1.28.2: dual AGPL/commercial, classifier 'Other/Proprietary License') was folded into PyMuPDF.
     -> Importing PyMuPDF forces the library to be AGPL-3-compatible and forces corporate users to accept AGPL (or buy an Artifex licence), adds 63 MB plus a second onnxruntime, and pins Python >=3.11; drop it from the default install.
  23. [high] Permissive PDF alternatives exist with Windows wheels: pypdfium2 5.14.0 (2026-10-04, BSD-3-Clause/Apache-2.0 over Google's BSD PDFium, 3.95 MB py3-none-win_amd64 wheel, no runtime dependencies) renders pages (page.render(scale).to_pil()) and extracts text (page.get_textpage().get_text_bounded()); pdfplumber 0.11.10 (MIT) builds on pdfminer.six 20260107 (MIT, 6.6 MB, pure Python, layout analysis) and pypdfium2>=5.9; pypdf 6.21.2 (BSD-3, 0.41 MB pure Python) extracts text/metadata. None of them perform OCR.
     -> Use pypdfium2 as the single PDF engine for both text extraction and rasterising low-text pages for OCR (same scanned-page heuristic as the PRD), with pdfplumber optional for table-ish layouts.
  24. [high] Office formats: python-docx 1.2.0 (MIT) exposes paragraphs, tables and iter_inner_content() in document order but excludes text inside revision marks and nested tables (text boxes/footnotes are not in its API); openpyxl 3.1.5 (MIT) reads .xlsx/.xlsm with load_workbook(read_only=True) streaming (must close()), not legacy .xls; python-pptx 1.0.2 (MIT) reads text via shape.has_text_frame -> text_frame.paragraphs with tables handled separately; xlrd 2.0.2 (BSD) reads legacy .xls only. No pure-Python readers exist for .doc/.ppt.
     -> Extraction matrix: .docx->python-docx (paragraphs+tables+headers/footers via sections); .xlsx/.xlsm->openpyxl read_only (cell values, sheet names); .xls->xlrd; .pptx->python-pptx (text frames, table cells, notes slides); .doc/.ppt/.odt->index filename+metadata only and flag 'unsupported'.
  25. [high] extract-msg 0.56.1 (Aug 2026) is licensed GPL-3.0; nested messages appear as EmbeddedMsgAttachment with type == AttachmentType.MSG and .data returning an MSGFile (so recursion is straightforward); its optional 'image' extra pins Pillow<10, which conflicts with Pillow 13.
     -> Because the core .msg parser is GPL-3, the library's own license must be GPL-3-compatible (GPL-3 or AGPL-3) unless you replace extract-msg; never install extract-msg[image]; recurse into AttachmentType.MSG with a depth limit.
  26. [high] .eml attachments need no third-party code: email.parser.BytesParser(policy=email.policy.default).parse(fp) gives get_body(preferencelist=...), iter_attachments(), get_filename() and get_content(). Pillow 13.0.0 (2026-10-16, requires Python >=3.11, 7.3 MB win_amd64 wheels cp311-cp315) reads PNG/JPEG/BMP/GIF/WebP and multi-frame TIFF (n_frames/seek); libtiff is compiled into the wheel (features.check('libtiff') returned True locally), so Group4/LZW fax TIFFs decode.
     -> Image attachments: open with Pillow, iterate frames, convert to RGB/RGBA, downscale to the engine's max dimension, then OCR; .eml via stdlib with the same attachment recursion as .msg.
  27. [high] Python-version envelope of the recommended stack on Windows: Pillow 13, onnxruntime 1.31 and PyMuPDF 2.0 require >=3.11; winrt-* wheels cover cp39-cp314; tesserocr cp39-cp315; torch cp310-cp314; paddlepaddle tops out at cp313; rapidocr-onnxruntime <3.13.
     -> Declare requires-python >=3.11,<3.15 for the library and test the OCR extras on 3.11-3.14; everything in the recommended chain has binary wheels across that range.


### Verifier
recommendation_holds=True

**Revised recommendation:** The core recommendation survives verification: use Windows.Media.Ocr (winrt-Windows.Media.Ocr + five companion winrt-* packages, ~0.9 MB total, MIT, no downloads, measured 0.12-0.13 s per A4 page at CER 0.17% in two independent runs) as the default OCR backend when OcrEngine.AvailableRecognizerLanguages covers the project's language, ship rapidocr 3.10 + onnxruntime 1.31 as the offline `[ocr]` fallback (bundled Apache-2.0 PP-OCRv6 small models, 52 routed languages incl. en/de/fr/es/it/nl/pl/pt/ja/zh, Microsoft-signed onnxruntime DLLs, measured ~2.7-3.6 s/page CER 0.03% on a desktop CPU), drop easyocr/PaddleOCR, and use pypdfium2 instead of PyMuPDF. Apply these corrections: (1) Microsoft's namespace docs say Windows.Media.Ocr is 'only supported for desktop apps with package identity (MSIX)'; it works from an unpackaged Python process today (verified twice) but is formally unsupported, so the library must treat it as best-effort and always be able to fall back to rapidocr. (2) Budget the `[ocr]` extra at ~110 MB, not ~90 MB (opencv-python-headless resolves to the 43.8 MB 5.0.0.93 wheel). (3) The easyocr stack is ~250 MB of wheels (not 190-225) plus 91-106 MB of GitHub-hosted weights; the case against it is stronger than stated. (4) PyMuPDF 2.0 does not bundle a second onnxruntime; it depends on the PyPI onnxruntime package and grew to 63 MB mainly from ~50 MB of bundled layout ONNX models. Remove it for the AGPL/commercial licence and size, not for a duplicate runtime. (5) 'No pure-Python .doc/.ppt readers' is false: sharepoint-to-text (Apache-2.0) exists; evaluate it (and docx2python for headers/footnotes/text boxes) before declaring legacy formats unsupported. (6) If the product owner will not accept GPL-3 in the dependency tree, python-oxmsg (MIT) is a candidate replacement for extract-msg that needs a parity check. (7) Engineering caveat: the researcher's combined benchmark that ran winrt OCR, a blocked tesserocr import and rapidocr/onnxruntime in one process segfaulted at the rapidocr stage in my rerun as well; isolate OCR backends (separate worker process or lazy, exclusive loading) and test the combination before shipping. (8) Keep tesserocr off the default path: 28 of its 29 Windows DLLs are unsigned and it is blocked under Smart App Control on the test machine; corporate WDAC behaviour is plausible but unverified. (9) Optionally expose onnxruntime-directml via rapidocr's use_dml as an `[ocr-gpu]` extra for the Radeon 760M; benchmark before promising any speed-up. (10) Declare the library's supported Python range as 3.11-3.14 on Windows (Pillow 13/onnxruntime need >=3.11; winrt wheels stop at cp314).

  - CONFIRMED: 25. extract-msg 0.56.1 (Aug 2026) is GPL-3.0; nested messages appear as EmbeddedMsgAttachment with type == AttachmentType.MSG and .data returning an MSGFile; it
  - CONFIRMED: 26. .eml needs only stdlib email (BytesParser(policy=default), get_body, iter_attachments, get_filename, get_content); Pillow 13.0.0 (2026-10-16, py>=3.11, 7.3 
  - CONFIRMED: 27. Python-version envelope on Windows: Pillow 13, onnxruntime 1.31 and PyMuPDF 2.0 require >=3.11; winrt-* wheels cp39-cp314; tesserocr cp39-cp315; torch cp310

### Missed Options

  - sharepoint-to-text 2.0.1 (Apache-2.0, pure Python, 2026-10-03): native readers for legacy .doc/.dot/.ppt/.pot/.pps/.xls/.rtf and .msg with no external tools; closes the researcher's stated .doc/.ppt gap (quality untested) - https://pypi.org/project/sharepoint-to-text/
  - docx2python 3.7.1 (MIT, 2026-08-06): extracts headers, footers, footnotes, endnotes, comments and text-box text that python-docx's API omits; useful alongside or instead of python-docx for attachment indexing - https://pypi.org/pypi/docx2python/json
  - python-oxmsg 0.0.2 (MIT, from the python-pptx author) and other permissive .msg readers: a route to remove the only GPL-3 dependency (extract-msg) from the library if the product owner cannot accept copyleft; feature parity (RTF-compressed bodies, nested .msg, property coverage) must be checked - https://pypi.org/pypi/python-oxmsg/json
  - onnxruntime-directml 1.24.4 + rapidocr `use_dml: true` as an optional `[ocr-gpu]` extra: the only pip-installable path that could use the Radeon 760M iGPU for OCR on this laptop (DirectML EP, 25 MB, cp311-cp314, MIT); speed-up on RDNA3 iGPU is unmeasured - https://pypi.org/pypi/onnxruntime-directml/json
  - mammoth 1.13.0 (BSD-2-Clause, 0.06 MB): robust .docx -> text/HTML converter that handles more document structures than python-docx for plain-text indexing - https://pypi.org/pypi/mammoth/json
  - markitdown 0.1.9 (MIT, Microsoft, 2026-10-21): one-stop docx/pptx/xlsx/pdf/msg/eml -> Markdown converter; heavier deps (magika, requests) and <3.15 Python cap, but a pragmatic fallback converter - https://pypi.org/pypi/markitdown/json
  - oneocr 1.0.12 (MIT wrapper, 2026-02-22): drives the Windows 11 Snipping Tool OneOCR engine (returns per-word confidence, newer model than Windows.Media.Ocr) but requires copying Microsoft's proprietary oneocr.dll/oneocr.onemodel from the Snipping Tool AppX package; not redistributable and not reliably readable by a standard user, so NOT viable for a PyPI product - noted only to explain why it was excluded - https://pypi.org/project/oneocr/
  - OcrEngine.TryCreateFromUserProfileLanguages() as the language-selection primitive for the Windows OCR path (picks the best installed OCR language from the user's profile instead of requiring the project language to match exactly) - https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/uwp/api/windows.media.ocr.ocrengine
  - Windows App SDK / Windows AI 'TextRecognizer' API: Microsoft's newer on-device OCR, but limited to Copilot+ PCs (40+ TOPS NPU); the Ryzen 5 8640HS (XDNA 1, ~16 TOPS) does not qualify, so it is not an option for the target hardware - noted for completeness



---

## Area: web-ui


### Summary

The pip-only, no-admin, zero-telemetry constraints are all satisfiable today with a plain ASGI stack: Starlette/FastAPI + uvicorn are pure-Python wheels (~0.3 MB combined, no telemetry code, native WebSockets and streaming for LLM tokens), and the UI can be a prebuilt SPA compiled at wheel-build time in CI and shipped as package data (hatchling `artifacts`/setuptools `package-data`), exactly as JupyterLab, MLflow, marimo, Khoj and Open WebUI already do. Of the Python-native UI frameworks only NiceGUI (FastAPI underneath, all Vue/Quasar/fonts bundled, no telemetry found in the wheel) passes an air-gap audit out of the box; Streamlit and Gradio phone home by default (usage stats, version checks, external-IP lookups) and Reflex needs Node 22+/Bun and has telemetry on by default. Localhost security must be built in: bind 127.0.0.1, validate Host/Origin (DNS rebinding), and gate the API with a per-launch token delivered as a cookie (the Jupyter/marimo pattern); Chrome 142's Local Network Access prompt helps but does not cover loopback-origin pages or Firefox. Launch is `python -m pkg` -> uvicorn on a free port -> `webbrowser.open` (os.startfile on Windows, no admin); optional extras can add a pywebview window (WebView2 runtime is preinstalled on Windows 11; pythonnet needs .NET Framework 4.8.x which ships with Windows 11) and a pystray tray icon. Background sync can be registered as a user-level Task Scheduler task (`/rl LIMITED`, the default) with a per-user Startup-folder shortcut as fallback; the PRD's chromadb dependency itself has PostHog telemetry on by default and must be disabled.


### Recommended

Build the UI as a prebuilt single-page app (Vite + Preact/Svelte, or htmx+Alpine if the team prefers server-rendered templates) compiled in CI at wheel-build time and shipped as package data (hatchling `artifacts` or setuptools `package-data`), served by a Starlette/FastAPI app under uvicorn with `websockets` for the chat stream (SSE via sse-starlette as the default, WebSocket optional). The server binds 127.0.0.1 on port 0 (or a configured port with fallback), generates a per-launch token exchanged once for an HttpOnly cookie, validates Host/Origin against loopback origins, sets docs_url=None, and `python -m emailsupersearch` opens the browser via webbrowser; `[window]` (pywebview/WebView2) and `[tray]` (pystray) are optional extras. This is the pattern marimo, JupyterLab, MLflow, Khoj and Open WebUI use, it is the only approach with a verified zero-telemetry, zero-CDN footprint, and it keeps the end-user install to `pip install emailsupersearch` with ~3-4 MB of server wheels plus a few MB of static assets.


### Alternatives

  - NiceGUI (3.18.0) — Python-only UI with FastAPI/uvicorn underneath, bundled Vue/Quasar/fonts and no telemetry, left drawer + @ui.page routing fits 'projects + left search pane'; costs ~8 MB wheel/27 MB disk plus python-socketio/aiohttp and you must override host='0.0.0.0' default to 127.0.0.1 and avoid CDN-backed widgets (AG Grid enterprise, Plotly maps).
  - Dash (4.4.1) — telemetry-free and serves assets locally by default; but Flask/callback model, plotly dependency (~37 MB disk) and dashboard idiom make a chat + projects UI awkward.
  - Streamlit (1.66.1) — fastest to prototype, sidebar/multipage built in; but usage stats, data.streamlit.io fetches and checkip.amazonaws.com lookups are on by default and the rerun model is poor for streaming chat and background ingest.
  - Gradio (6.30.0) — chat components out of the box; but 30 MB wheel/79 MB disk, analytics on by default and an unconditional version check to api.gradio.app at launch.
  - Reflex (0.10.0) — nice React output but requires Node 22+/Bun for developers, two processes at runtime, telemetry_enabled=True; only usable by pre-exporting the frontend in CI, at which point a plain SPA is simpler.
  - Flask + waitress + htmx (SSE only) — smallest mental model and Windows-proven (MLflow uses it); but no WebSockets with waitress and you re-implement the security middleware MLflow had to retrofit.
  - pywebview-first 'desktop app' — same Starlette backend but opened in a WebView2 window instead of a browser tab; depends on pythonnet/.NET Framework and WebView2 presence, so keep it as an extra not the default.


### Install Footprint

Recommended (Starlette/FastAPI + uvicorn + websockets + sse-starlette + pydantic): fastapi 0.14 MB + starlette 0.08 + uvicorn 0.08 + h11 0.04 + anyio 0.13 + websockets 0.20 (cp311-cp315 win wheels) + httptools 0.09 (optional) + sse-starlette 0.02 + python-multipart 0.03 + pydantic/pydantic_core ~2.7 MB => roughly 3.5 MB of wheels, plus your compiled SPA (typically 1-5 MB; precedents: khoj 16 MB, jupyterlab 17 MB, mlflow 27 MB on disk). Optional extras: pywebview 0.5 MB + pythonnet 0.2 MB; pystray 0.05 MB. PRD option: the PRD names no UI framework (CLI/Excel/pyvis HTML only); if the implicit choice were Streamlit it is 9.9 MB wheel / 30 MB on disk before its pandas/pyarrow/pillow/altair dependency tree; Gradio 29.9 MB wheel / 79 MB on disk; NiceGUI 8.1 MB / 27 MB; Dash 8.5 MB / 37 MB; Panel 19.7 MB / 67 MB. For scale, the PRD's chromadb alone is a 23.5 MB win_amd64 wheel and Open WebUI (a comparable pip RAG UI) is a 101 MB wheel / 250 MB on disk.


### Open Questions For Owner

  - Browser tab vs. app window: is a plain default-browser tab acceptable, or do you want the optional pywebview/WebView2 window (and a tray icon) as the primary experience on the office laptop?
  - Port policy: random free port each launch (most secure, URL printed/opened automatically) or a fixed port (e.g. 8765) so users can bookmark it? Should an already-running instance be reused (single-instance lock)?
  - Which browsers must be supported on the corporate image (Edge only, or also Chrome/Firefox)? This affects whether you can rely on Chrome's Local Network Access prompt and on modern CSS/JS features.
  - Do you accept a Node.js toolchain in CI/dev (never for end users) to build a React/Svelte SPA, or must the UI be Python-only (NiceGUI / htmx templates)?
  - Python version floor: 3.11 (needed by starlette 1.x and keeps Open WebUI-style 'py<3.13' pins avoidable) vs 3.12/3.13 - and which Python distribution can the standard user actually install (python.org per-user, Store/install manager, or an IT-provided one)?
  - Will the corporate GPO allow a user-level Task Scheduler task for background sync, or must the fallback be a Startup-folder shortcut / 'sync while the UI is open'? Can this be tested on the real image before design freeze?
  - Multi-user laptops: is it enough that the token stops other local users/tabs, or do projects/indexes need per-Windows-user isolation and ACLs under %LOCALAPPDATA%?
  - Where should 'projects' live (one SQLite/Chroma folder per project under the user profile?) and should a project be exportable/shareable as a folder?
  - Is a 'no outbound sockets ever' CI test an acceptance requirement (it would catch chromadb/huggingface/version-check regressions in transitive dependencies)?


### Claims

  1. [high] Verified on PyPI 2026-10-23: fastapi 0.162.0 (uploaded 2026-10-23, pure py3-none-any wheel 0.14 MB, requires-python >=3.10); starlette 1.9.0 (2026-10-18, 0.08 MB, requires >=3.11); uvicorn 0.54.0 (2026-09-25, 0.08 MB, >=3.10); sse-starlette 3.5.0 (2026-09-28, 0.02 MB); h11 0.16.0; anyio 4.15.1. All are pure-Python wheels, so no cp311/cp312/cp313 win_amd64 wheels are needed.
     -> The core server stack installs anywhere pip works; pin starlette>=1.x only if you accept a Python 3.11 floor.
  2. [high] uvicorn's optional native accelerators have Windows wheels: httptools 0.9.0 ships win_amd64 wheels for cp39-cp315; websockets 17.3 ships win_amd64 wheels for cp311-cp315 (plus a pure wheel). uvloop is excluded on win32 by the `standard` extra's marker ("uvloop>=0.15.1; sys_platform != 'win32' ..."). `standard` also pulls watchfiles, PyYAML, python-dotenv.
     -> Depend on `uvicorn` + `websockets` (and optionally `httptools`) explicitly rather than `uvicorn[standard]`, to avoid dragging in watchfiles/dotenv/pyyaml you do not need.
  3. [high] uvicorn defaults: --host 127.0.0.1, --port 8000; "If set to 0, an available port will be picked." --ws accepts auto/none/websockets/websockets-sansio/wsproto; --forwarded-allow-ips trusts only 127.0.0.1 and ::1 by default.
     -> A random free port is a one-liner (port=0); read the bound port back from the server socket before calling webbrowser.open.
  4. [high] Starlette has a native WebSocket class/WebSocketRoute, StreamingResponse (FastAPI docs: takes an async or sync generator and streams the body), StaticFiles(html=True, packages=[...]) that serves index.html for directories and can read assets from inside an installed Python package, TrustedHostMiddleware (invalid Host -> 400) and a CORSMiddleware whose defaults are "restrictive by default" (allow_credentials False, allow_methods ['GET']).
     -> Everything needed for an SPA host + SSE/WebSocket token streaming + Host validation is in Starlette itself; FastAPI adds only routing sugar/pydantic validation.
  5. [high] Wheel audit (downloaded and grepped 2026-10-23): fastapi, starlette, uvicorn, flask, aiohttp and pywebview contain no analytics/telemetry endpoints (starlette and litestar only ship optional OpenTelemetry middleware modules). However FastAPI's and Litestar's auto-generated API docs load Swagger UI/ReDoc from cdn.jsdelivr.net/unpkg.com by default; FastAPI docs: "By default, those files are served from a CDN" and "To disable them, set their URLs to None" (docs_url=None, redoc_url=None).
     -> Set docs_url=None/redoc_url=None (or self-host the three static files) so the only external reference in the server layer disappears.

[high] Flask 3.1.3 (2026-02-19) streams via generator responses ("Each yield expression is directly sent to the browser") but has no WebSocket support; flask-sock 0.7.0 (last release 2023-10) adds it for Werkzeug/Gunicorn/Eventlet/Gevent only (waitress, the usual Windows WSGI server, is not listed). Litestar 2.25.0 (2026-10-18, 0.56 MB) has built-in ServerSentEvent/Stream responses and websocket_listener/websocket_stream handlers. aiohttp 3.14.5 "supports WebSockets out-of-the-box" but run_app binds 0.0.0.0 when no host is given and add_static is documented "for development only". stdlib http.server "is not recommended for production", defaults to HTTP/1.0, has no WebSocket support (ThreadingHTTPServer 3.7+, HTTPSServer only in 3.14+).
     -> SSE alone works on every option (even Flask/wsgiref with chunked responses); if you want WebSockets for bidirectional chat/ingest progress, pick an ASGI framework (Starlette/FastAPI/Litestar) or aiohttp, not Flask or stdlib.

Default: true"); Streamlit staff confirm the data goes to Segment and stops when set false. The bundled frontend also references https://data.streamlit.io/metrics.json and static.json, and streamlit/net_util.py calls http(s)://checkip.amazonaws.com (get_external_ip) as part of its CORS origin allow-list logic. Default fonts (Source Sans/Serif/Code) are bundled locally.

Reflex compiles a React frontend served on :3000 talking over WebSockets to a FastAPI backend on :8000; release notes for 0.9.9 state Reflex requires Node 22.22.0+ and Bun is the default package manager. `reflex export --frontend-only` can emit a static frontend.zip, but the self-hosting docs warn "self-hosting alone does not make an app air-gapped".

Dash 4.4.1 (2026-07-21, 8.5 MB) serves component bundles locally by default (`serve_locally` True since Dash 1.0, documented as necessary "in firewalled or airgapped environments"), has no telemetry, and only hits unpkg.com when serve_locally=False (geo maps still fetch cdn.plot.ly unless a local topojson is supplied).

A custom build hook (hatch_build.py, BuildHookInterface.initialize(version, build_data)) runs "immediately before each build" and can append to build_data['artifacts'] or build_data['force_include']; hooks can be disabled per environment (enable-by-default=false, HATCH_BUILD_HOOK_ENABLE_<NAME>, HATCH_BUILD_NO_HOOKS) and can declare their own `dependencies`.

JupyterLab 4.6.4's wheel (16.4 MB) carries 17 MB of prebuilt static assets, and user docs say only source extensions "require Node.js to rebuild JupyterLab".
